I think that the second simple message that we are actually sending with this draft, and hopefully quickly, will focus on symbols in particular. The purpose of writing this draft was to regulate the use of symbols that justify four generally accepted crimes: crimes against humanity, war crimes, acts of aggression or genocide. And I believe that, in international law, there is no other way or ambiguity than genocide, which is a crime against humanity, a war crime or an act of aggression. The way courts finally appreciate it, of course, is different. In theory, people can interpret it differently in their own vision. I dare say that all four have been committed in Ukraine and continue to be committed. Perhaps the reasons for regulating the use of symbols that justify these crimes are more than enough. And there's no way for the state to say no, we're not dealing with it or, oh, we already have laws. No, we must also say, if necessary, as an educational provision and as a preventive provision and, of course, as an applicable provision, what we really mean. We actually think that the use of such symbols is forbidden. Reading the alphabet is not conceivable. Really, symbols change. Just as, for example, in the Ukrainian resistance struggle, a sunflower has become a symbol, it is a resistance struggle and its symbol. So giving a clear message in this sense is, in my view, extremely important. However, I would like to dwell on what was left out of this bill. No, it's not some kind of legal crime or whatever word was used here. The Constitution is in force in the Republic of Estonia. The second paragraph of § 12 of the Constitution explicitly states: "Instigation of national, racial, religious or political hatred, violence and discrimination is prohibited and punishable by law. Says the same thing: "It is also prohibited and punishable by law to incite hatred, violence and discrimination between social groups. " I do not count from the constitution that there should be an additional clause. There must be a risk to the life or health of a person. But the Penal Code today says that incitement to hatred, which is mentioned in § 12 of the Constitution, is punishable only if it poses a risk to the life or health of a person. The Constitution doesn't say that. We are afraid of this public debate in society for some crazy reason. We're always scared. We fear an argument or fear the validity of the Constitution. Hey, you idiot! The Constitution is valid in Estonia. The Constitution says that any incitement to hatred is punishable. There's no need to do this. And that is why I am sad that this provision has gone out right now in war conditions. But I understand this need for compromise because, above all, it is necessary to give a signal from Parliament, and a rapid signal that it is indeed the symbols justifying war or going to war on the aggressor's side that are prohibited and, if necessary, punishable. But I call on us to realise that this is a right given to the police, not a norm that must always and always be used. The police must assess each time whether the use of one symbol or another is intended to justify the four crimes. I believe the Estonian police are smart enough. The Estonian police are not a traffic camera that simply detects, fixes and imposes a fine. The Estonian police are self-thinking and are able to make decisions on whether or not it is necessary to punish or enough to tell someone, please take away this Georgi tape or please remove the Z-letter. And if a person doesn't react, it's possible to punish him. So, dear friends, I sincerely call on all of us to deal with this bill very quickly. The Estonian state must assert itself if it is necessary. And I believe that the Estonian state can do this, and the Estonian parliament can do it, and we will be able to adopt these two rapid changes by agreement as early as next week. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U21	Let's move on to item 16 - First reading of the draft law amending the Employment Contracts Act initiated by the Conservative People's Party of Estonia. I ask for a presentation of the Riigikogu speech chair of the Riigikogu member Anti Polandmets.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U22	Thank you. If the previous, quite rightly the above-mentioned draft, raised many questions and we have an undefined legal situation where there are many question marks, this draft is specific, substantive and useful. It is a draft amendment to the Employment Contracts Act [the Act] which lays down the principle that holidays provided for in the Employment Contracts Act are supplemented by days of national holidays and public holidays falling on weekly holidays. Additional days of rest shall also be extended to the minor's annual leave, the annual leave of a member of staff with partial or absent capacity and the leave of an educational worker. State holidays are 10 according to the Holidays and Holidays Act. Five moving public holidays will fall this year. An extra holiday is not given for a public holiday, which is always on Sunday. The Central Party and the Social Democrats have also supported the granting of an extra holiday for a public holiday on the weekend. The logic of the draft is the same as in previous drafts: giving extra holidays, because it seems unfair to many if the state's promised holidays cannot be realised. This draft allows the employer to compensate for the days so that they are added to the annual leave. The Social Democrats recently came up with a bill that says that holidays would have been immediately compensated, but it is more complicated because the employer cannot plan its activities so abruptly. The principle is, I think, correct for the thoughts of both the Socialists and the Centre Party, but it is easier to add these [days] to the annual leave. What do you need it for? We know from the world that the Americans are working extraordinarily hard, they have a much shorter vacation than we do. Nevertheless, Estonia employs considerably more than Finland, Sweden, Denmark and Norway. At the same time, our people are in a poorer state in terms of their life expectancy and healthy lives. Occupational health experts find that workers need longer leave to recover. In time, it means 20 and more days. During this period it is possible to rest off from fatigue and tension. Longer holidays have an impact on the growth of job motivation and contribute to better health. According to 2019 data, men 54 and women 57 years of age lived healthy in Estonia. The number of healthy years has seen a positive change in rural areas. While in Estonia the average life expectancy of men was 73 years in 2018 and for women was 82 years, in Sweden it was 80 years for men and 84 years for women. Maybe we have a lot to think about. Public health is, in my opinion, a goal worth pursuing. We know that in Soviet times, the most important thing was to implement the plan, and whether they worked themselves to death was secondary to the state. I think we should do the opposite, see that we are healthier, stronger and lead a more meaningful life, not a mysterious five-year plan in three years, keeping ourselves, keeping our descendants, doing more self-development, maintaining our health, health sports. It's all necessary. If we move forward with the extension of holidays, maybe someday we will get even further and allow more freedom from slavery to families with children. Let's take these steps, even small steps, gradually. Of course, people must have the opportunity to work, but in my opinion, at least mothers of families with children should have the choice of staying at home. Now, this is the right maternity leave, if the mother of three or four children can stay home and feel like a real mother, you don't have to work in Maxima. I do not think that [the last mentioned] is a big and sacred goal. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U51	Thank you, Mr President. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! Estonians have genes working to kill themselves. "Stand to work, late to bed," "Do your work and work" and countless other words of wisdom praising industriousness have been reverentially passed on to us from generation to generation. However, a comparison of the countries of the European Union shows that we have the full power to kill ourselves with our work. In fact, our working person is a sovereign leader among the Member States in terms of hours worked each year. Statistics bring together the number of working hours, the duration of annual leave and the number of public holidays free of work. According to 2018 data, a virk and an Estonian worked 1848 hours, while a Frenchman escaped in 1602 hours and the so-called average European in 1714 hours. True, as we do, the almost 40-hour working week also applies in half of the Member States of the European Union. It is also true that a short annual leave similar to our leave is still available in a dozen Member States. There's not a lot of public holidays, ten, but that's not quite an exception. Let's put aside the holiday season of some southern countries and take a look around the neighbors for a second. There are 12 public holidays in Latvia, for example. The same was true in Lithuania, and from 2020 they took on another day to celebrate. There is a public holiday in Finland 13, in Sweden 14. Additional holidays for a public holiday on a weekend are given in, for example, Belgium, Bulgaria, Spain, Ireland and Luxembourg. In Malta, public holidays during the weekend are partially compensated. So, in the case of Estonia, a long week of work, a short year's leave and a limited number of public holidays coincide, and even these small ones go to water when they get to the weekend. Years aren't brothers, sometimes it's better, sometimes it's not one bit. This year, we have been especially blessed: in 2022, there will be five more holiday holidays that mean an extra day of free time - January 1st, May 1st, August 20th, Christmas Eve and Christmas Day 1st - either on Saturday or Sunday. The fact that our working people deserve more holidays has been mentioned in Estonian politics for years. The Social Democrats have also repeatedly come up with legislative initiatives, but they have collided with the confrontation and rhetoric of right parties about how we are not yet so rich and how the Estonian economy is going to suffer. I'm glad we've taken a step forward by now. Our draft, which aims to give people an extra holiday on Monday for a national holiday or a public holiday, passed the first reading in the big hall. We hope that we can also adopt the bill as a law this spring season. Then we'd take a few breaks in the big job, all together, as a country. In many countries, the law provides for a free Monday when holidays fall on the weekend. In some places, this regime applies only to the public sector and private operators can decide for themselves whether to grant their employees a rest day or half [days of rest] in this case. In other parts of Europe, a series of measures are in place to prevent people from losing their breath. In some places, a regular holiday is accompanied by a holiday, as set out in the EKRE draft; on the other hand, a paid holiday is given to the employee at the same time as a public holiday. Much may be transferred to the so-called bridge system to preserve the integrity of the week. But every last breath we need is desperate. After all, all EU work and rest surveys show a strong link between shorter working hours and labour productivity and worker satisfaction. A person with a well-balanced work and private life has better health and greater well-being than others. Satisfied life, in turn, affects the employee's dedication and performance. It thus benefits the employee, the employer and society as a whole. More joy at work, at home, and at rest would be good for the whole community. The Social Democrats support the EKRE draft. Thank you.

In 2020, an amendment was introduced to the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act, which was part of the major draft COVID. At that time, the conditions of termination were decided, including the examinations. The Government of the Republic made changes to the national curriculum and graduating from school was not related to the result of the exam. The following year, in 2021, an amendment was made to the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act, according to which the condition of graduating from upper secondary school was not to pass a state examination, an internationally recognised exam replacing it, a school examination at upper secondary school and a student study or practical work. However, an amendment was made to the national curriculum of comprehensive school without changing the law, according to which a comprehensive school student who has passed the final exams required for completion of comprehensive school under the Basic School and Upper Secondary School Act, regardless of the outcome of the exam. As can be seen from the list, the details may be different, but the content is generally one thing: whether and how to check students' knowledge when completing comprehensive school.

The threshold, the satisfactory result is, I think, for every student to be able to. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U169	Liina Kersna, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U170	Thank you, good questioner! I'll check to see if the microphone works. It's working. You can hear me. Indeed, as of yesterday, 2888 children [of war refugees] have been registered in the Estonian education system, 70% in general education, 22% in early childhood education, 5% in upper secondary schools and 3% in vocational education. If you look at the language of instruction, 67.6% of all these children have gone to the Estonian education system, 11.5% to kindergartens and schools based on the language immersion method, and 20.3% have gone to the Russian education system, or Russian kindergartens and schools, as of yesterday. 17 children, or 0.6%, have gone to education in English[in the system]. The country has said from the very beginning, from the beginning of the war, when we knew that war refugees would come here, that it is very important that children of war refugees are primarily directed to the Estonian education system. We also supported [the] Ukrainian community's own recommendation that these children go first and foremost to education in the Estonian language [in]. We see today that more than 67% are in the Estonian-language education system, but 20% are in the Russian-language education system. I don't think more than 20% should be allowed to grow that percentage. And why? Why is it important that children are in the Estonian educational system? In particular, because in the Estonian language environment, children learn the national language considerably faster in Estonia. We look at the results as the second language of the Estonian language of Russian-speaking schools; they are dramatic. They're really very, very bad. We have not been able, and here, of course, it is worth looking in the mirror at all of us who have been in government, why we have not been able to ensure enough Estonian-language education in Russian-speaking schools. [The consequence] is that, for example, only 40% of children in the fourth grade did at the most basic level, the A1 level exam of Estonian as a second language, and only 19% did the part of speaking; they managed to pass the part of speaking. It is therefore very important for children to be in the Estonian-language education system so that they can learn the Estonian language as quickly as possible. In fact, when communicating with schools today, these Estonian-language schools, where children have reached, as well as kindergartens, say that children will blend in very quickly, already learn the Estonian language nicely and can handle it. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U173	Liina Kersna, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U174	Thank you. I'll pick up where I left off. We monitor very closely the areas in which children are directed to Russian-speaking schools. In Tallinn and Ida-Virumaa, where there are more children in Russian education, we communicate and ask whether this [decision] is at the request of [a] parent. There is such a law in Estonia that the parent's wish is decisive where the child is directed. If a parent says that he wants his child to go to a Russian educational establishment, then the local government, if he has places in such a school or kindergarten, must also offer this place. We have learned of isolated cases where parents have said that we wanted to be in Estonian, but we were still directed to the Russian language. The Ministry of Education has responded to these cases and we have managed to solve them so that children have received education in Estonian[institution]. We are in constant contact with these local authorities and monitor them. Investment in Estonian-language education. For example, [we invest] in support of the "Plus One Teacher" programme, because we know that there are already many other native-language children in Estonian-language schools today. A language immersion methodology is a research-based methodology to quickly integrate a child into the Estonian-language education system. The number of these schools is likely to increase, and we certainly need to support these schools so that they can do well. I am pleased that today Estonian schools have already recruited 80 military workers from Ukraine. 30% of them work as teachers, including Ukrainian language and literature teachers, as well as mathematics and class teachers, and 39% of them have been recruited as teachers' assistants. So I am sure that Ukrainian military refugee educators are also an important resource who can help in schools.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U239	The only acceptable part of the law that was adopted yesterday was that of the Ukrainian refugees, which could, moreover, have been regulated differently. However, this draft has been launched in January and does not concern Ukrainian refugees, but we will indirectly improve the situation of Ukrainian refugees on our labour market with the same draft. So it would now be possible for those ambassadors who voted in favour of this defective draft yesterday, of course, to at least partially rehabilitate themselves by supporting this draft, I hope that the President will not announce it. The aim of this draft is to direct employers to recruit foreign workers, in particular, for those jobs that require qualified workers. On the basis of valid fixed-term residence permits and registration of short-term employment, working foreigners in Estonia currently make up 3 to 4% of the employed, which is, I will hurry up a bit, the circuma one and a half times less if we have registered unemployed, who were over 46000 as of March. In addition, we also have Ukrainian refugees, of whom the circa 2000 is of working age, I cannot say exactly. They have received temporary protection and can also be employed against a minimum wage. Of these, 3215 had been registered as unemployed yesterday. Okay. The number of job vacancies per person registered as unemployed is 0.2, about one per person registered as unemployed. The aim of this draft is to limit the flow of immigration only to qualified workers, those of whom there is a shortage in the Estonian labour market. The second objective is to rule out the possibility of working with domestic workers and now the Ukrainian refugees being replaced by lower-paid workers employed abroad. As you know, there are a number of exceptions in the Aliens Act which are not subject to this wage criterion, which is proposed in the draft. This bill does not change that. For example, a teacher, a research worker, a posted worker, a domestic worker, a start-up employee, an employee who has obtained a qualification or a higher education in Estonia, is not subject to the wage criterion proposed in this draft. In short, there are many exceptions to which this wage criterion does not apply, and these exceptions are precisely laid down on the basis of the needs of the Estonian labour market. Of course, this draft does not in any way restrict the labour migration of citizens of the European Union or persons residing in the European Union on the basis of their permanent right of residence. This means that immigration of workers with the average wage required for Estonia is essentially not limited and does not restrict the immigration of specialists, because we need good specialists and it would be illogical to think that the average salary of a good specialist is less than 1.5 Estonian average wages. Once again, this bill does not restrict the recruitment of refugees in Estonia, but because it restricts the recruitment of other foreigners, it indirectly creates even a better situation for refugees in our labour market. Thus, the first objective of the draft was to raise the average wage requirement to 1.5 of the Estonian average as well as to increase the wage requirement for short-term workers to 1.5 of the Estonian average as a general rule of labour migration. All right, let's go. The draft reduces the period of short-term employment allowed in Estonia to six months instead of 270 days for seasonal workers who may be paid the minimum wage. According to the definition of a government regulation, the season must be linked to a fixed period of time per year when demand for labour is significantly higher due to the season than at other times. It doesn't make sense that the season itself is longer than the rest of the season. The Annex to this regulation, where the activities considered to be seasonal work are counted, is in itself contrary to the definition of the season, as there is also the production of non-alcoholic drink in addition to agricultural work. Well, is the production of non-alcoholic beverages really a seasonal job? Obviously not. I consulted agricultural scientists who said that there was no seasonal work in agriculture that would last more than six months. Maybe somewhere in the south there's a longer period of vegetation, but we don't. The second half of the draft is the amendment of the Higher Education Act. The draft complements the Higher Education Act by requiring all foreign students who have a legal basis for temporary stay for study in Estonia or who have obtained a temporary residence permit for study, with the exception of nationals of a Member State of the European Union, of a Member State of the European Economic Area, of the Swiss Confederation or of a Member State of the OECD Organisation who study at first or second level. Unless these, say, third-country nationals study very much in Estonian, they do not need to be charged tuition fees. I would like to point out that, of course, neither the citizens of the European Union nor the doctoral candidates should be required to pay the costs of studies. It would be logical that we do not apply the obligation to reimburse the costs of studies immediately, but only the next or perhaps the following academic year. So there's time. After the first reading, we could propose another amendment to this draft, so that it does not concern Ukrainian students, as is currently the case. Okay. In addition, the bill regulates the time when a foreign student may invite a spouse to live with him or her and when the residence permit of a person who has taken up the student will be revoked if that person simply no longer studies here. This time was not regulated before, according to this bill it is 30 days. Okay. These provisions of the Law on Higher Education are amended for the reason that our universities do not become migration pumps. Okay. As you can see, this is a very fundamental draft, although the draft itself might need to be updated in the light of new processes and adopted legislation. One of the things that really is here is that Ukrainian students should not be affected by it. On the other hand, immigration is always fresh. Now I'm gonna explain to you why. I'm sure we've repeatedly put forward these arguments, but I'm offering them to you once again systematically, grouping these four groups. First, the first group, the economic arguments. Economics is known to distinguish three groups of factors of production or economic resources: natural, economic and human resources, including entrepreneurship. According to the principles of the market economy, the optimal combination of these factors of production is the basis for the organisation of rational production, for progress. This is the way in which choices are made that ensure that economic activity meets the opportunities and needs of society, as well as innovations in the economy. Okay. Changing one production factor from above, precisely to allow this cheap influx of labour, I am not talking about a highly skilled workforce, but precisely about the introduction of cheap and medium-paid labour, which deviates from the principle of a market economy. If an analogy were used, for example, the introduction of cheap labour would be the same if our country were required to produce cheap raw materials, raw materials that are cheaper than the established price level. What would we call it? The claims that we are somewhere like this, and so many workers are missing, are completely incomprehensible in this sense that if you bring me a hundred employees, let's say who works for a very small salary, I'll put them to work, and I'll arrange this company. Such a deviation from the market economy principle favours the activities of companies using cheaper labour at the expense of other companies and does not in any way favour renewal. I am not blaming entrepreneurs here because they are going this way, which is easier for them. This is the basic concept of business. However, it is our duty here to create a framework for entrepreneurship that will guide entrepreneurship towards innovation. I would like to point out here that our average labour productivity is only four fifths of the euro area average, which means that if we raise average labour productivity to an average level in the euro area, which should happen when we modernise the economy, we will get more than 150,000 more workers. Forward, social policy arguments. I recall that the proportion of our population of foreign origin, which comes from the period of occupation, is still one of Europe's largest. We have taught them, with great effort and time, some of those people who have a second mother tongue to speak the Estonian language, and their social and political behaviour is somewhat closer to that of the indigenous population, however, let us say that only somewhat. The integration of the population of foreign origin into Estonian society as an objective is still relatively far away. We now also have Ukrainian refugees to deal with. But if the mass immigration continues, as has been the case so far, it will all become negligible. At least to some extent, security, people-to-people trust and cooperation also suffer in our society. All right, let's go. Wage growth in Estonia has been relatively rapid, but if we look at real wages, we must say not so fast. The rise in prices will absorb wage growth, and the import of labour will certainly slow wage growth. As I said before, we have 46000 registered unemployed. As unemployment decreases, the burden on the state budget will actually decrease. Onward, more about the social policy arguments. After all, every immigrant who has come to live permanently here as a working age uses social services, and let us not forget that his or her non-working family members also come with him. The employment rate of immigrants is usually well below the average. Estonia's pension costs, let's not forget, are now so high because of past immigration, because the birth rate in the eastern Slavic countries is lower, and if Estonia has a higher birth rate than in the eastern Slavic countries, and the pattern of birth rate behaviour there is brought to Estonia, just as it has happened and is happening now, that means that we are creating a higher tax burden on our children. Thus, the promotion of immigration is at the expense of the future of our children. Thirdly, security policy arguments. I don't think I need to talk about this for a long time. It is reasonable to believe that in the case of such mass immigration as it has been, not all those who arrive can be controlled. I think that would certainly be necessary now, because there is a flood of refugees, they need to be controlled. But as extensive a mass immigration as we have had should be narrowed down so that refugees can be received properly. Fourthly, if you like, national policy or constitutional arguments. These relate in particular to the extent of immigration. In recent years, we have moved 4 000 to 5 000 people from the countries of Eastern Slavic culture and from Asia and Africa, mostly more than they have left. These are the data of the Estonian Statistical Office, which may be adjusted. I don't have any better data to take, nobody has any better data to take right now. We are waiting for the results of the census, we are waiting for the data for 2021, but according to preliminary reports from Statistics Estonia, it can be assumed that the order of magnitude was similar to the previous one in 2021. I'm pointing out there's no count of refugees here. A positive migration balance of this magnitude means mass immigration, that is what it means. This same lower level of wages caused by immigration hinders the return of our people from abroad, to which they have been led by an earlier incorrect economic policy. Already in 2020, according to Statistics Estonia, a positive trend turned to the other side, which means that 6900 Estonian citizens left Estonia and 5900 returned. This trend has turned. If we continue with such low-cost labour and immigration, it will certainly deepen. We do not know more about the latest data, but we do know that the share of Estonian-speaking people in the population fell in 2021, before the arrival of Ukrainian refugees. Yes, check the Estonian Statistical Office's [page], where the "Estonian 2035" action plan measures are available: the share of Estonians has fallen. So each of these four groups of reasons alone would be enough to be in favour of this bill. If anything needs to be changed, it is possible to propose improvements. To conclude, the majority of society clearly does not want mass immigration. At the same time, I expect this to be one of the key issues in the next parliamentary elections. In subsequent elections to the Riigikogu, political parties who have promoted mass immigration will not be voted for. It is logical to believe that the seats of the Riigikogu will be reduced in these government departments because they have promoted mass immigration. There are fewer seats for them, a large part of them cannot and it is difficult to find work elsewhere, because there are few vacancies due to mass immigration. So coalition ambassadors should have a totally personal motive to vote in favour of this bill. Thank you very much.

In other words, on a global scale, the number of people dying from these avoidable side effects, or medical errors, is over 3 million each year. This is a serious [problem] and for no reason has the World Health Organisation paid very serious attention to it. Until now, there have been different systems of handling medical errors in different countries. We have a 'criminal' system under criminal law and civil law, which has not really provided any solutions. It is not about punishment, it is about having as little of these mistakes as possible, so that doctors are not afraid to talk about what is happening and try not to hide them in the future. Sweden was the first to introduce a non-formal system in 1975. This means that the sufferer will receive his or her compensation, but it does not have to be guaranteed to find a specific culprit, but it will be dealt with separately. This system has been in Finland since 1987, Norway since 1988, Denmark since 1992, Finland already has a new law. I'll have more time, please.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U342	Three minutes extra time. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U343	Canada, Australia, New Zealand have the same system. Our neighbours are also ahead of us: Latvia has a so-called medical risk fund from 2015 and Lithuania from 2020. What we want is essentially a cultural change. What we want is for us to be satisfied with our heart and to know that it is safe to be sick and that we cannot get anything more than our illness. That is why this law is extremely important, the law that I hope we all support. But it's just the beginning, it's only two years from now, and there's a lot of work to be done. Changing the entire internal culture of medicine is a long process and will never end. So it's an important bill. For ten years we have been waiting for this and not just waiting, but actually working on it. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U347	Good chairman of the sitting! Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I ask you to support the draft law on mandatory liability insurance for health care providers. This law has long been awaited by the Association of Patients, the Association of Hospitals, the Association of Doctors and the Association of Nurses. The need for compensation for vaccine damage came on the agenda acutely after some people suffered health damage following the administration of the COVID-19 vaccine. The purpose of this bill is to create a state fund for the Estonian Health Insurance Fund, from which people can apply for compensation up to 100000 euros. The benefit can be claimed both in case of damage to the COVID vaccine and in case of any medical defects resulting from the use of other vaccines. For the purpose of knowing that there are cases to be reimbursed where there is at least a likelihood of a link between the damage to health and the vaccination. This means that the people of Estonia do not have to prove in any way that one hundred percent of the damage to health is related to vaccination, but is a question of probability. Such a system of vaccination damages already exists in 27 countries, such as Finland, Sweden, Denmark and elsewhere. I am very pleased that we now also have the opportunity to join the ranks of countries that defend their people with dignity. This draft also defines patients' liability insurance, which will help patients to apply for compensation more easily in a situation where a health care professional has found a medical error. In this case, the patient can apply for benefits from the insurance and does not have to go to court. This is certainly very necessary from the perspective of our patients. I would like to warmly and sincerely thank Minister for Health and Labour, Tanel Kiike, who has really worked out a draft law on the Vaccine Damage Fund in a few months, and this is before us today. Please support this bill. This will help our people and give them the opportunity to receive compensation due to medication errors or the adverse side effects of vaccination. Thank you.

One thing that was raised here as a problem was the binding question for a preliminary ruling that the Council of Rectors wanted. Supposedly, Jaak Aab, Minister of Public Administration, had promised that it would become a law. As a matter of fact, Jake Aab has not promised it. Indeed, the Council of Rectors submitted a binding draft preliminary ruling, but just before that Act of 2017. The idea was that a preliminary ruling could be used in an analogous way, as can be done in the tax administration's case under the Tax Administration Act, whereas the Public Procurement Act does not allow such a binding preliminary ruling to be made. This is the conclusion of the analysis by the Ministry of Finance. In this regard, we did not have this topic in this debate, could not have been, but since it was introduced in the Chamber, I have further examined how this should be. Alternatively, the Public Procurement Act provides for the possibility of coordinating procurement procedures at the request of the contracting authority by the Ministry of Finance so that, if the procurement is agreed in advance, there will be no change. After all, the Ministry of Finance has been reviewing these procurement procedures a great deal over the years and can give its coordination on this basis. The second point was the issue of the procurement of the COVID tests. Indeed, as I said at second reading, it is not possible to complete the details due to ongoing monitoring, but it is possible to use the negotiated procedure without prior publication. Now, let's get on with the fast-track change of contracts. It was pointed out that we have difficult times and that the procurements we have already concluded are very problematic. In this respect, the Ministry of Finance already published on 22 March a guide on the possibility of changing contracts based on the price index or, for example, on the consumer price index, and also recommended that the value indexing of the contract be linked in new public procurements. In a difficult situation, in fact, the contracting authority itself is also able to come to the other party, tenderers or contracting partner, to accept that it makes payments on the basis of its contract earlier, helping to deal with such crisis situations. The very big issue that was raised here was the entry into force of the law. Yes, it can be discussed whether it should be June 1st or may be later, three months later. But I would say that, first, amendments to the draft were started early. The Ministry of Finance started introducing them in February last year. The changes have been regularly introduced in training and seminars, most recently at a public procurement seminar organised by the training market.ee on 24 March, where these changes were resolved all day. There will also be another training on 21 April in the Teams environment, where the number of participants will certainly be quite high. So basically, the preparation has been very long-term and thorough. I would like to remind you once again that, among the current amendments, there is also a point which determines that public procurement concluded until 1 June or the adoption of the draft will be completed according to the old rules. So it's like there shouldn't be a problem here. The problem was, of course, the internal audit of the Ministry of the Interior. There, too, was the subject of the wording in the area of security. It was simply their internal auditor who dealt with the situation much more strictly than the law required. So, to sum up the subject, I would simply say that this amendment is aimed precisely at solving the practical problems with which contracting authorities and tenderers have run into public procurement, and this problem has been resolved with great consensus. In offshore parks with an eight-gauge capacity, 32 terawatt-hours of electricity per year could be produced, which would be almost four times the consumption in Estonia in recent years. Both households, companies and exports will continue to benefit from favourable energy at the Estonian sea. These amounts are higher than the annual budget of Estonia. One aspect of the draft is still very important. In particular, we changed the methodology for calculating the building charge between the two readings. In determining taxes and national charges, simplicity, clarity and market-relatedness are values in themselves. When the law enters into force, the building charge is calculated at 1,5% of the net turnover of sea wind parks, which means that if the wind energy potential is developed and the energy price is EUR 50 per megawatt-hour, then the state budget will receive a building charge of 240 million per year for the use of the sea area alone. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U418	I thought they changed the glass. Thank you. Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, I will introduce you to the proceedings on this draft in the Committee on Culture, which took place on Monday 4 April this year as a second item on the agenda. Commanded by Aadu Black, chairman of the commission. Members of the committee Helle-Moonika Helme, Jaak Juske, Signe Kivi, Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits, Tõnis Mölder, who was Marko Šorin's alternate, Heidy Purga, Üllar Saaremäe, Margit Sutrop and Jaak Valge. Daniel Raudsepp, Deputy Secretary-General of the Arts of the Ministry of Culture, Karin Ligi, Legal Adviser and Audiovisual Adviser Siim Rohtla were invited. The draft introduced Üllar Saaremäe, who also did so here in depth. So I'm not going to read the minutes, but to his introduction, the representative of the Ministry of Culture, Daniel Raudsepp, added his explanation. He said the following. The Media Services Act already limits the display of several Russian television channels on Estonian soil. It's much more complicated with Internet channels. Some of the online portals in Estonia are limited, based on the Electronic Communications Act and the Penal Code. He also pointed out that the printing media is also a channel from which the same publications spread the propaganda of war to Estonia. Both Russian magazines and newspapers come to our attention. He mentioned that Omniva has actually taken certain steps on its own initiative to limit the distribution of publications, but it has not been possible to restrict it entirely. I would also like to read out a letter from the Ministry of Culture to us earlier. It was sent on 4 March by Legal Adviser Karin Ligi, and it says: "The Media Services Act has the means to prevent television programs containing hostile propaganda, and making such a proposal to the Government of the Republic does not lead to the inevitable need to change laws according to the Ministry of Culture. Existing measures can in principle always be supplemented, but, as has already been said, there are bases in the current Media Services Act to limit the freedom to receive and retransmission of television programmes. " I read it because it was available to the Commission, but it is not currently in the Commission's protocol. The following discussion, questions were asked, attended by Heidy Purga, Üllar Saaremäe, Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits, Margit Sutrop, Jaak Valge, Aadu Black and the speaker before you. Heidy Purga asked if it was true that the law did not provide a means of proactively preventing propaganda channels from entering the market, and what would be the easiest solution to be proactive. Aadu Black added to this the question of whether it is necessary to amend the law or whether it may be delegated, for example, to the level of government regulation or other standard. Karin Ligi then pointed out a text that had already been read out and stressed that the Ministry of Culture's earlier written statement that there was no need to amend the law applied to the text of a specific draft and to the explanatory memorandum, where there was a reference specifically to the restriction of television channels. He reiterated that these possibilities really existed in the Media Services Act and also said that it was always possible to foresee additional measures requiring the amendment of the Act. Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits stressed that she did not agree with the position of the Ministry of Culture, everything was not okay. He gave an example of Estonian hotels where you can still watch propaganda channels, even though they are banned in Estonia. Jaak White asked how many Russian television watchers had been in the past and how many could there be now. We didn't get an answer to that. But if you promise, I will also read out that Jaak Valge considered it necessary to add that he does not represent the views of his party when he states that he has doubts about the draft, because Estonia is a free democratic state, there is free distribution in Estonia and the opportunity to freely spread the truth from his side to his people. He asked if it was really fear that we in Estonia would be under the influence of propaganda. Daniel Raudsepp replied that yes, it is a philosophical problem and it is difficult to explain philosophically from the surface, but in fact the problem is wider and we all understand that it starts with school and language learning. But he agreed that, as Viktoria Ladynskaya-Kubits and Jaak Valge pointed out in their questions and opinions, this is a complex whole problem and a proposal for analysis is justified in every way, because this analysis was sought. The speaker here debated with Jaak White and found that Estonia is a free democratic state, but it is important to inform society that we have legislative measures against propaganda and the symbols, defamation and lying of the aggressors. Let's keep going. Margit Sutrop now added to the previous debate that, if only to prohibit the transmission of false facts and information, this is not enough, as there is a very wide-ranging exercise of influence. And he expressed his concern by referring to a specific proposal for a draft law that it was a long-term process and so long the propaganda already in place could go very far. This should not be underestimated, so that action should be taken decisively and quickly. We really discussed the draft and this problem in a much broader way, and Mr Must, the chairman of the committee, reminded us that there was a debate on a specific draft. He found that a substantive analysis of the media area had taken place, involving the Ministry of Culture, Estonian Public Broadcasting and also international organisations. And as the previous speaker has already mentioned, the chairman of the committee also said that he himself does not consider it necessary to vote in favour of the draft in this case. Jaak Juske reacted to this, who felt that the draft was still topical, because this debate referred to it. We must do everything to better prevent, restrict and prohibit anti-Estonian propaganda. Yes, indeed, freedom of expression is a very important freedom in a democratic country, but this freedom of expression can also be used against democracy, emphasised Jaak Juske. Aadu Black said that a majority of the composition of the Riigikogu would be required to adopt the draft as a decision. The Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu has confirmed this need and it was available as a text to the members of the committee. I know it's possible to get acquainted with it in this room today. The chairman of the committee proposed to appoint a speaker before you to represent the Commission, to propose a draft on the plenary agenda on 12 April, to propose a final vote on the draft, and to repeat once again that a majority of the composition of the Riigikogu is required to adopt the draft as a decision. On its side were Aadu Must, Signe Kivi, Jaak Juske, Üllar Saaremäe, Heidy Purga, Margit Sutrop, Tõnis Mölder, Helle-Moonika Helme and Viktoria Ladynskaja-Kubits. Jaak White abstained. Then the Commission's advisor Urvo Klopets intervened, who added, insofar as it was overlooked, that linguistic and technical changes had been introduced to the draft text. They're very small, but very important. Technically, the preamble to the draft has been organised and the language part of the text: "Estonia's hostile," two different words, has been replaced by the text section "Estonia's anti-Estonian." On this point, Jaak White asked for a new vote, as he announced that he was in agreement with this text. And Aadu Black cancelled the Commission's vote, put the draft back to the vote, and it was therefore decided: to propose a draft on the plenary agenda on 12 April 2022, the decision was taken by consensus; to propose a final vote on the draft; the decision was taken by consensus; to appoint Signe Kivi as the representative of the lead committee, the consensus. The committee voted that a majority of the composition of the Riigikogu is required for the adoption of the draft decision. This too reached a consensus. Thank you for your attention.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U463	Ladies and gentlemen, I have to tell you today about political gambling in connection with this bill, unfortunately. Time around us gives us an obligation to think very carefully how far we as politicians, looking for attention to substitute actions and half-sisters, at times with false claims, are willing to go and the risks we take in our society. Perhaps it will help those who, blindly trusting their most experienced colleagues or party leaders, have gone slippery for a moment to think about the nature and appearance of things. I am certainly not addressing Mr Kruusimäe here, because watching him porridge about what Kaarel Oja is doing in the word of God is a place where they say Father, forgive them, they do not know what they are doing. But I come to the point. I'm sorry to burden you with some facts and a time series. The processing of the draft law amending the Media Services Act and other laws here in the Riigikogu was blocked for a very long time by the joint forces of the two parties, Isamaa and EKRE. As a result, Estonia was able to transpose the European Union Audiovisual Media Services Directive into its law very late and it only came into force after Russia had started a massive brutal war on 24 February. More specifically, the president announced it on 25 February. Then TTJA also reacted and it was published in the Official Journal of the State on the 27th. While the date of entry into force of the provisions of the Directive was 19 September 2020, while Isamaa was an active member of the government, Estonia was only able to do so one and a half years later. This delay also meant that Estonia's outdated Media Services Act, which was based on the obsolete directive of the European Union, did not include the important standards that were included in the revised Audiovisual Media Services Directive of the European Union. It was during the Estonian Presidency of the Council of the European Union in 2017 that, with the help of the likes of thought, the mechanisms for regulating the distribution of these cross-border media services were significantly modified in the directive being renewed, making it much more favourable than before to the country of destination of media services from another Member State and allowing the interests of the country of destination, its security, the stability of society and the information space to be protected more effectively and quickly. For a long time, Estonia was unable to make use of the much better means available in the directive to protect its interests and effectively restrict aggressive channels of influence with Russian content in our information space, as the old Media Services Act did not provide good opportunities for this. But the transposition of a new, much better mechanism in the Riigikogu was long prevented by several hundred stacked proposals for improvement. It was already launched in February 2021, which, unfortunately, directly damaged Estonia's interests at the time. For some reason, in the draft amendment to the Media Services Act, two parties highlighted only one provision, § 19: extending the prohibition of incitement to hatred to incite violence and terrorist offences, addressing the context and the meaning of the law externally and creating a wave of hysteria around it, with the support of which they exhibited themselves as hot defenders of freedom of expression and media freedom. At the same time, while a reasoned update of the implementation of the hate ban under arbitrary interpretations was hindered, the transposition of Article 3 of the EU AVMSD, which was thoroughly amended, was also prevented, allowing a Member State to act in the defence of its security and stability as soon as the first serious infringement was detected in a cross-border media service. All our neighbours, Latvia, Lithuania, Finland, Sweden and others, had long understood and transposed the need for the rapid implementation of the revised norms of this directive in their national law, but in Estonia it was obstructed for a determined long time, thus weakening the country's ability to act decisively in defending its interests in our information room. And when a dubious compromise was found on the extension of the prohibition of hate speech in § 19 of the Media Services Act after a long period of silence and an arduous search, an addition was added to it, which in fact weakens the content of this rule and limits the possibilities of implementing it in restricting cross-border media services. True, the revised Media Services Act now has the mechanism that was fought out during the Estonian Presidency of the Council of the European Union and which allows to act vigorously and prevent the spread of media services from another Member State in Estonia if it is found that it threatens the security and defence of our country and, more generally, the stability of society. This is a good and important addition, which we have now, although very late, but in the end, taken over. At the same time, the addition to § 19 of the revised Media Services Act, which is the compromise you have requested, significantly limits the scope for controlling and restricting brutal hate speech in media services from another country. Why? Because even if, for example, in some of our foreign television channels, hatred and violence are encouraged on ethnic grounds, on the basis of race, gender, language, political beliefs or birth, or any other form of group character, our national supervision would only be able to limit the spread of this television channel in Estonia if it has been proven that the incitement of such hate speech poses a threat to a person's life, health or property. Unfortunately, in real life, proofing such direct links is difficult and often impossible for foreign media services. But it's the case law. Therefore, we cannot now apply the ban on incitement to hatred and violence as effectively as possible in practice and, on this basis, really restrict the channels of aggressive propaganda with Russian content, for example, friends. Why would you necessarily add that addition? You could say he's still unclear. Was it not possible to foresee and understand the harmfulness of this arbitrary supplement, its weakening effect in protecting Estonia's interests in our information space and ensuring the stability of society? Well, I don't think the former Minister for Justice and Foreign Affairs, Urmas Reinsalu, could understand that. In his political experience! All the more so as he was the Minister of Justice just as Estonia, as President of the Council of the European Union, and the Baltic and other colleagues, defended this point. He had to be informed in essence, although the negotiator was the Ministry of Culture, the responsible minister was me. But Reinsalu has always closely monitored the content of such draft laws and directives. Russia's brutal war in Ukraine and the systematic use of controlled media by the Putin regime as a weapon and tool in the preparation, execution, justification and concealment of Kremlin aggression should also make us think about the need to amend our rule of law so that we really have the best up-to-date and effective tools to protect Estonia's interests, which directly affects what is happening in our information space and, more generally, throughout the country. The government should therefore repair as soon as possible the scrap that was introduced into the Media Services Act as a result of the cynical campaign of EKRE and Isamaa and as a result of the submissive behaviour of the government. If this draft decision would lead to this, then we could even vote in favour of it. Finally. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U491	Honourable representative of the people! Nor, of course, is it a question of what decisions we make in the defence logic regarding the operational plan. This means that the territory will remain unprotected from the opponent's missiles and from the air strikes that the territory will not be exposed to, where the front logic will actually take place in the event of the full-blown attack scenario being realised, and so on. The Commander of the Defence Forces is not autonomous in the sense that the framework that the Commander of the Defence Forces can do will be proposed by the legislator, by a government. And then these are the decisions, the cruel decisions that the Commander of the Defence Forces has to make in a war situation, in fact, decisions that result from our actions or omissions. Certainly, the rules on budgetary balance cannot be a barrier and a constraint to this. We also propose to the government to seek a defence agreement with other countries of the European Union to double the defence capabilities of the European Union, which then means the total defence capabilities of the countries of the European Union. Of course, the European Union does not have a separate army and defence capability. The point is that, while during the Estonian Presidency of the European Union, which was well recalled by Indrek Saar, who spoke earlier here, we reached a political agreement that the countries of the European Union also have the aim of achieving 2%, in reality we could now aim at doubling the defence capabilities of the countries. ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U522	Honourable Chairman! Ladies and gentlemen, I will introduce you to the proceedings in the National Defence Committee. The Riigikogu Isamaa Group submitted a draft on 15 March of this year. The Bureau of the Riigikogu set the deadline for submitting the draft amendments at 5.15 p.m. on 29 March, but no amendments were tabled to the draft. The National Defence Committee discussed the draft before the first reading at its meetings on 4 and 5 April this year. Urmas Reinsalu, a member of the Isamaa Group, took part in the meeting on 4 April. Lt-Gen Martin Herem, Head of the Defence Forces, and Kristjan Mäe, Head of the NATO and European Union Department of the Ministry of Defence, were invited to the meeting of the Commission as experts. Urmas Reinsalu presented the idea and content of the draft, and then Lieutenant General Herem presented the Commission with regard to defence activities. In so doing, the Commander of the Defence Forces explained that the State's defence action plan relates both to national defence funding and the resulting capacity developments as well as to NATO-Allies' plans and contributions. The detailed information provided by General Herem was for internal use and was not included or reflected in the minutes in accordance with § 39 of the Riigikogu Home and Working Procedures Act. Kristjan Mäe commented on paragraph 3 of the draft, which relates to the principles of budgetary balance in the European Union, that the Government of the Republic has already adopted a position to support a similar distinction, but not to the extent of all national defence expenditure, but in such a way as to exempt defence investments from compliance with the rules on budgetary balance. As regards the provisions of point 4 of the draft act concerning the conclusion of a European defence agreement, the Mäe explained that it is important to distinguish whether we are talking about the defence capabilities of the European Union or the Member States of the European Union. The defence ambitions of the European Union for the coming years have just been agreed between the Member States and endorsed by the EU Council on 21 March in a document called the Strategic Security and Defence compass. The Estonian and Eastern European countries have supported the principle that all European countries spend at least 2% of their GDP on national defence, but unfortunately not all Member States still do so. Member of the Commission Heiki Hepner stated that the fourth point of the draft is for the purpose of doubling defence capabilities to the countries of the European Union, not the European Union. The debate on the draft decision continued at the meeting of the National Defence Committee on 5 April this year, where the author was represented by Mr Heiki Hepner, Member of the Commission. On 16 March this year, Aivar Engel, the Head of the Secretary-General of the National Defence Commission, delivered an opinion on the draft, who stated, inter alia, that paragraphs 1 and 2 set out the terms relating to the national defence action plan (the draft term "Estonian Defence Plan'), which is based on § 7 (2) to (5) of the National Defence Act. This is a military strategic document containing secret state secrets, which is not included in the strategic development documents referred to in §7 (1) and (11) of the National Defence Act, §19 (1) (3) and §20 (1) and (2) of the State Budget Act, which are submitted to the Riigikogu for approval or discussion, or to the National Defence Committee for a position. In addition, § 7 (11) of the National Defence Act states that the obligation laid down in § 20 (2) of the National Budget Act to submit it to the Riigikogu for discussion is not applicable to the development plan in the field of national defence, which is essentially a national defence development plan. Before submitting a development plan or a draft amending it to the Government of the Republic for approval, the Prime Minister listens to the position of the National Defence Committee of the Riigikogu. However, the strategic plan for the defence activities of the State is an even lower-ranking military document, which is not a strategic development document. The state defence action plan is linked to the NATO defence action plan for Estonia. They are separate documents, but they are linked to each other and are presumably implemented together. This was supported by all but one of the members of the Commission. The same was true of the definition of "Estonia's defence plan" in the draft text versus the definition of "state defence plan" in the current law. Members of the Commission considered that they were probably of the same meaning. Here are some of the doubts that we had in the discussions at the National Defence Committee. The title of the draft is "Modernisation of the Estonian military defence plan" and it follows that this plan is currently in place, which is wanted to be completed. Point 1 of the draft law requires the government to submit an action plan to supplement Estonia's defence plan within one month. Point 2 requires an improved protection plan within one month, the end result of which, in turn, the requirement for an action plan within the same timeframe becomes meaningless. It was also noted that since the defence plan is secret, it is not known whether or not the defence plan is sufficient. Although the initiators of the draft have used the term "military defence plan" in the draft, which is understood as a national defence action plan in the field, the Commission had the impression that the initiators of the draft had rather as a complement to the defence plans, that it meant supplementing the national defence development plan. Point 2 aims to double national defence spending. The question arose: if today there is a real percentage of 2.5, is the target 5%. As far as the European Union is concerned, the fourth point is also the proposal to double the European Union's defence capabilities, which seems to be an unrealistic objective, as the European Union's strategic compass does not include such ambition, and European countries have not even been able to fulfil the commitment made at the NATO summit in 2014 to allocate 2% of GDP to national defence. Under parliamentary supervision, the National Defence Committee wishes to review the National Defence Development Plan in an up-to-date form, as several development jumps have taken place in the meantime thanks to additional funding. The Commission proposed to invite a representative of the executive in this field, the Minister of Defence, to the National Defence Committee to get an overview of the improved national defence development plan. Such activities are directly part of the tasks of the National Defence Committee and there is no need to ask the Government of the Republic for such information through the Grand Chamber of the Riigikogu. Next was the draft debate as a decision of the Riigikogu on the majority necessary for adoption. The Executive Board of the Riigikogu, by its decision of 15 February 2022, instructed the Commission that when dealing with a draft decision of the Riigikogu containing a proposal to the Government of the Republic, it is necessary to ask the Government in a letter whether the adoption of the bill would lead to an amendment of the law. At the same time, the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu must be asked to give an opinion on whether the draft requires 51 votes for adoption or whether a majority of votes in favour is sufficient. From the opinion of the Legal and Analytical Department of the Chancellery of the Riigikogu on 1 April and from the opinion of the Secretary-General of the National Defence Committee of 16 March this year, it follows that the draft under discussion requires a majority of the composition of the Riigikogu for adoption. From the point of view of the Minister of Defence, pursuant to the mandate of the Government of the Republic, on 11 April this year, it is assumed that it is not necessary to amend the laws in force when adopting the draft under discussion. However, the agreement on defence cooperation under point 4 of the draft must be ratified by law in the Riigikogu. Paragraph 4 of the draft provides for the conclusion of an agreement on defence cooperation, which is directly related to § 121 (4) of the Constitution, which states that the Riigikogu will ratify the treaties of the Republic of Estonia with which the Republic of Estonia will assume military or material obligations. In addition, § 103 (2) of the Constitution provides the Riigikogu with the right to appeal to the Government of the Republic on the basis of a decision taken by a majority of its component members [proposal] to initiate a desirable draft, which would be the agreement on defence cooperation arising from section 554 (4) of the draft law on ratification. The three opinions referred to above are publicly available in electronic form on the draft under discussion. To sum it up. The plan of the defence activities of the state is the same as that of the Estonian defence plan, which is secret and is not subject to discussion in the Riigikogu on the basis of the National Defence Act and the State Budget Act and is not submitted to the National Defence Committee to give a position. The Riigikogu approves the foundations of Estonia's security policy pursuant to § 11 (1) of the State Budget Act and, as I mentioned in my report, the National Defence Committee gives its position to the secret national defence development plan pursuant to the National Defence Act. Point 4 of the draft under discussion results from the submission of the draft ratification law, which is why the draft under discussion requires a majority of the Riigikogu's membership to be adopted as a decision of the Riigikogu. Following the above, the National Defence Commission took the following decisions on the draft at its meeting on 5 April this year. Not to support the bill. There were 8 members of the Commission in favour of this, against 1 in favour of Heiki Hepner. The consensus decisions of the committee were to propose to the Riigikogu Board that the draft under discussion be included on the Riigikogu's agenda for the first reading of this year, to appoint a speaker as rapporteur and that the draft requires a majority of the Riigikogu's membership for adoption. Thank you!
