ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U579	The tenth item is the presentation by the Minister for Enterprise and Information Technology of the implementation of the country's long-term development strategy "Estonia 2035." Mr Andres Suti, Minister for Enterprise and Information Technology, would like to speak as rapporteur.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U580	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable Riigikogu! We are building an Estonia that matters to the world. We're on our way to the next peaks. Before going to the development strategy "Estonia 2035," let me go back in time to the early 90s. We had just become an independent country. We had fought for freedom to shape our own future just as we dared to imagine it and set ambitions. It was a time of anxiety and uncertainty, both at national level, in our immediate neighbourhood and in the wider world. Anxiety and uncertainty [exemplify] today: the health crisis, the energy crisis and Russia's aggression against Ukraine, and the changing geopolitical lines of power frame the implementation of the "Estonia 2035" strategy, and have accelerated the need for progress. In the nineties, together, we managed to overcome the crises at the time and build an outstanding success story in Estonia, which speaks to the wider world. We can do this by implementing the Estonian 2035 strategy today. Good Riigikogu! The "Estonia 2035" strategy sets one goal: the Estonian economy is strong, innovative and responsible. In addition, the second strategic objective related to my area of government is that Estonia's government is reliable, human-centred and innovative. To implement these goals, there are two central development plans that direct the activities of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. The first is the R &amp; D &amp; I &amp; entrepreneurship development plan 2021 to 2035 or TAIE, the second is the development plan for the digital society of Estonia 2030, which sets out a vision, an action plan how to develop Estonia as an economy, state and society through digital technology over the next decade. Estonian labour productivity has reached 84% of the European average in the last 30 years, which is a very good level. Our starting point is about one sixth below the European Union average. In order to reach the R&D&I target, labour productivity must increase by more than a quarter. We have rightly set our ambition high: 110% of the European Union average, above the current level of Finland and Germany. This is a challenge for both the government and the private sector. To achieve this level, one of the keys is to keep the state's R &amp; D costs at least 1%, which we do. The level of private R &amp; D expenditure is currently just below 1% of GDP, i.e. we need a double increase in private R &amp; D. I am optimistic because we have companies in every county of Estonia that are innovative and operate in very different fields. And there is a strong interest in cooperation between companies and universities. At the same time, we cannot overlook the fact that the Estonian population is ageing and by 2035 our population is projected to shrink to 1.2 million people. Over the next five years, about 31000 people in the age group 20 to 64 will be reduced. A recent report by the Riigikogu Monitoring Centre stated that our tax system today will no longer withstand demographic trends, which is why we should start introducing new taxes. However, a much better solution than tax increases is to increase the growth potential of the economy than it is today. Thus, the state's revenue base is growing and there is no need for an increase in the tax burden. Instead of greater redistribution, we create much more value by encouraging innovation and the creation of high-paid jobs. This is where my main challenge, as the Estonian economy and minister, is to achieve a strong innovative and responsible growth of the Estonian economy despite the demographic situation. Simply put, how can we make the economy flourish so that our welfare system does not collapse? In the Ministry, we have framed the actions to address this challenge between the three strategic directions. These include a digital turn, a green turn and an innovation turn. The fourth and horizontal axis is the business environment. I will now open these four issues a little bit longer. The innovation revolution involves the transition to a new smart and research-intensive economic model, with innovative and environmentally friendly products-services created by top-level managers and employees. In other words, Estonia focuses on high value added quality, not volume. Our competitive advantage is not in cheap mass production, but in the provision of high quality products and services, often as tailor-made solutions and short delivery times. In order to make an innovation turn, we have focused on four lines of action. Firstly, to create, develop and market new innovative products, we bring together Estonian universities, research institutions and companies. This is what we do with applied research and other programmes. We have already invested EUR 41 million and another 53 million will be added in the next four years. Secondly, Estonian start-ups contributed nearly one billion euros last year. In the first three and a half months of this year, the sector has involved almost the same amount. In order for our technology-intensive start-ups to be able to start and get started in Estonia, we will increase the availability of venture capital through new investment funds and accelerators. Through SmartCap, we invest 100 million euros in developing the ecosystem and modern technology-intensive industries of research-intensive start-ups, plus at least the same amount of private investment. Thirdly, in order for private investment in R &amp; D to increase to 2%, we will create new measures based on structural resources and public budgets. We also set the objective of investing 2% in R &amp; D for public undertakings in line with the owner's expectations. Fourthly, in order to ensure that we are not only consumers of new technologies, but also their creators, we support the participation of Estonian companies in the European Union's crucial value chain-based cooperation projects. A good example is the hydrogen sector, where Estonian companies contribute to the development of new technologies and innovation in strategic sectors and as partners they contribute to the development of the European Union's strategic value chains. Good Riigikogu! Next, it is appropriate to look deeper into another subject: the green turn. Addressing climate issues is not an option; it is a basic requirement. Success requires radical innovation at different levels of society. For companies, this requires not only the introduction of novel technologies, but a review of the entire business model, measurement and reduction of the environmental footprint. First, however, we need to see the sheave as an opportunity, not a cost. Climate warming, supply chain crises and consumer preferences have irreversibly changed the expectations and preferences of businesses and consumers alike. Swimming upstream is like fighting windmills. For example, the financial sector is paying ever greater attention to the corporate environmental footprint and the risks associated with it. More and more economic operators must be able to show either the supplier or the final consumer what the environmental footprint of the entire value chain of the product is. More and more companies are therefore restructuring their processes, supply chains, replacing technologies or changing business models as a whole. The green turn is, of course, wider than that of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. Both the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, responsible for energy, and the Minister for the Environment have a major role to play here. Our main activities for green turn are as follows: Firstly, in order to support companies active in new green technologies, both start-ups and start-ups, we created a green fund of EUR 100 million. It provides funding through equity investment at different stages of development and across sectors, creating new products or services based on green technologies. Secondly, during 2021, concepts of support measures financed by the Fair Transition Fund and aimed at enabling the so-called fair transition in Ida-Virumaa have been developed in order to achieve climate objectives. Large investment aid for Ida-Viru will also be opened in 2022, allowing sustainable investments in new enterprises and production units. In the second half of the year, additional research intensity support and support for investments by small and medium-sized enterprises will be opened. All these actions will support the development and implementation of new environmentally friendly solutions. Good Riigikogu! The third strategic direction concerns the digital revolution. Estonia has successfully digitised 99% of our public sector services. At the same time, the digitalisation of our private sector is below the European average and is holding back productivity growth. In this way, it is important to support the digital revolution, and for that we are planning the next steps. Firstly, with the help of automation, robotics and artificial intelligence to increase the competitiveness of the industrial sector, we support companies' investment in digitalisation and the introduction of robots at EUR 53 million. Secondly, in order to help the productivity of Estonian industry to increase and increase the achievement of these sustainable goals, we invest 56 million euros in total. Thirdly, in order to save money, nature and working hours, we create a new real-time business environment where administrative activities, financial transactions and reporting are carried out automatically between machines. It is estimated that the transition to the real-time economy would save around EUR 200 million per year and over 14 million hours of work. As a first step, we move to e-invoices, e-mails and e-invoices. In other words, we will make payment and supply chains faster for businesses and we will lose paper cheques in shops for citizens. To this end, we will invest EUR 2 million in support of businesses this year. Good Riigikogu! No strategy or plan is worth anything unless it is implemented. What do we need for this? To succeed in the triple turn, the Estonian business environment must be the best. So the business environment is the fourth and last thing I want to talk to you about. We have a lot of untapped potential. Ensuring investment certainty and smooth interaction with investors and businesses with a country where agencies are primarily solution-oriented advisors, not sanctioners, is a basic need, creating the best living environment and opportunities for citizens and talent for self-realization. First of all, the quality of management. Our activities in shaping the business environment are forward looking and take into account what our competitors are doing. Every euro that we invest in a triple turn is targeted and amplified. Last year, we decided to merge two main business support organisations, EAS and KredEx. At the beginning of this year, the integrated institution will play a key role in implementing the triple turn and simplifying the customer path of businesses. Second of all, the connections. We all know that everyday life today is increasingly taking place in the digital world, whether it be teleworking, education, or other activities. However, there are nearly 80,000 addresses in Estonia, which are without high-speed internet access. The COVID crisis highlighted these bottlenecks particularly painfully. Creating fast internet connections is one of my main priorities. This is why we will invest over EUR 70 million in high-speed internet access over the next three to four years. It's more than the last ten years together. With regard to the new support measure, we have been guided by the logic that targeted investments can only be made in cooperation with local authorities and not in the office of the Ministry of Economic Affairs. The establishment of a fast internet connection provides an opportunity for our companies, employees, children and everyone else to build Estonia's next success story from every corner of Estonia, so that even in the most distant village, no child's education will suffer because of the fluctuating Internet and no company's work will stop. Similarly, 5G data will finally reach Estonia this summer, which, in addition to the ultra-fast internet, will open a completely new horizon for our companies to bring solutions to the market in the future that we cannot even foresee today. Third, our digital state. One of Estonia's best known success stories and business cards abroad is our digital state and its image. For years, the Digikuvans have opened doors for us and our companies and have attracted investment and talent here. In recent years, however, our best known brand has been neglected at home. The digital state has been underfunded for years, and we have allowed our digital state to drown in the dungeon, while our own expectations of the digital state have increased. I am pleased that our government values the digital state and that this year's budget has made the first considerable investment in sustaining the digital state, with a total of 30 million over the last four years. The next development of the digital state is user comfort and focus. We have started activities to ensure that the services are proactive, that they function invisible to the user and that they are also accessible from the smart device. In the second half of the year, a road map will be completed to take the digital state to smartware, i.e. digikuru. In future, you can also participate in elections using a smart device. Development work is ongoing and when the work is completed on time, all tests confirm that this is a sufficiently secure solution, and so is the Republic Election Committee, then the first opportunity to vote in the next Riigikogu elections will arise at the earliest. Due to the image of a strong digital state, the e-residency programme has also been launched. Today, the resident of Estonia has brought over 20,000 companies to the state and brought some 29 million additional net income to the state budget last year. Throughout the programme, e-residency has brought 89 million gross revenues to the country. By 2025, more than 40% of new e-residents have started a business and the direct tax revenue of the Estonian state has increased by at least 30% annually. Speaking of dig, we can't ignore cyber security either. This is the other half of the digital coin, without which digitisation and the digital revolution are not sustainable, but rather increases our vulnerability. During the last day, 46 malware-infected websites or servers, 191 malware-infected devices, 13 service barriers attacks were detected in Estonia. But it's just the tip of the iceberg about what's going on in our cyberspace every day. Cybersecurity must be an area that gets more attention in both the public and private sectors and is adequately funded. Fourthly, we need to talk about trade relations. The COVID pandemic has shown the importance of security of supply and has been exacerbated by the energy security crisis. The biggest review of the global supply chains of this century is currently taking place, and Estonia must use this opportunity to our advantage. We have the opportunity to take Estonian companies to the supply chains of large companies in the world or to strengthen their existing position there. The more Estonia is embedded in the supply chains of large Western companies, the greater our international footprint and visibility, the more important our foreign partners think we are and the more important Estonia's welfare matters to them. During my term of office, I have made nearly 30 foreign visits, 13 of which were directly linked to the creation of business opportunities for businesses. I can only confirm that Estonia has a great deal to offer to the world and that there is a great deal of interest in us. But competition is also intense. Success depends on us, our ability to market, our ability to find partners, and our ability to scale where appropriate. This brings me to the fifth important point of the business environment, which is talent and skilled labour. It is not possible to digitise or robot production without an engineer. Similarly, it is not possible to move the digital society forward without developers. The Estonian labour market is shrinking by 31000 employees in five years, and we are already lacking in all sectors, including manufacturing, construction, IT companies and the service sector. The lack of skills is the biggest bottleneck for our companies across Estonia. In essence, all the companies I have met highlight the development of the workforce as the most limited company, regardless of whether it is an IT company in Tallinn or a furniture factory in Setomaa. We are looking for opportunities to ensure, in cooperation with the Ministry of Education and Research, the succeeding growth of talent in Estonia and the re-training of our people according to the needs of the market. Private sector initiatives are also worthy of recognition, such as Jõhvi [Technology] School or Cleveron Academy. We have supported the latter during the COVID with the Ministry of Education and Research. However, these activities do not provide a rapid relief to Estonia's skills shortages and in the case of professions that cannot be filled with Estonia's skilled workforce, we must look to foreign talent. Otherwise, we ourselves will pull the handbrake on the Estonian economy and lead ourselves to the ditch. I am therefore pleased that the Riigikogu passed the Aliens Act on Monday, which helps to alleviate the labour force problem in Estonia. It is wrong to claim that the changes to the law will result in a reduction in the income or loss of jobs for Estonian people. On the contrary, the more attractive Estonia is as a destination for investment, the more jobs will be created and the more incomes will increase. The final point is the image of Estonia abroad. In the case of exports and, in particular, foreign investment, what we have internationally and how open we are to raise capital as an economy and as a society plays a crucial role. Estonia is known mostly as a digital state in the world. Estonia is the first country to operate as an e-service. Estonia has started a number of world-renowned technology companies and we have more viable start-ups per capita than anywhere else in Europe. We are the first country to offer e residency, the first country where e-voting took place. In addition, we are the most enterprising country in Europe and tax declarations will take place in three minutes. And the Estonian industrial sector is gaining recognition and recognition. In relation to Estonia's image, we also need to talk about the hospitality sector. The hospitality sector has suffered the most from the recent crisis, had to adapt business models and has not yet recovered from the crisis. The country has been shouldered with 160 million grants and guarantees. Of course, no support measure will replace the market or the customer during the tourism season. I am sure, however, that the better days of the sector are ahead. Why? Because we have an excellent hospitality sector. The state helps to market Estonia and we have a lot to offer as a destination. The slogan of a fresh tourism strategy says: "The time has come. "Our value offer is quality tourism, world-class food culture, beautiful nature, exciting cultural experiences and convenient solutions for teleworking and business tourism. In recognition of Estonian food culture, the world famous Michelin label comes to Estonia. On May 25th, we will find out which restaurants got an asterisk and which food sites got into Michelin's guide. This is the highest recognition for the leaders of Estonian food culture. Last Friday, we invited Estonians and Finns to visit each other and rediscover the neighbouring country together with our Finnish colleague Minister Lintilä. My own final experience of the versatility and speciality of the Estonian guest sector comes from Kirsi farm in Setomaa. Kaidi Kerdt, with the roots of Setomaa, returned to Setomaa after several years of commuting between Estonia and abroad and founded his own tourism farm there. According to the hostess, market demand is many times higher than the capacity to meet it. And we've got more and more stories like that without telling us. Let's not keep them to ourselves, let's share, and then we'll have new success stories. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I look optimisticly towards 2035. I have visited every county during my term of office, met dozens of entrepreneurs and people. These meetings confirm that our people are looking to the future "glass is half full, not half empty" prism. Of course, we have bottlenecks in Estonia that need to be resolved. But together, we have also been through more difficult times and successfully overcome them. 30 years ago we started rebuilding a re-independenced Estonia. Even then, we had difficult times at national level and anxious times outside. But our eyes were shining, thinking about the future of Estonia. At the time, we dared to think big and act fearlessly. Let's make our eyes shine again to make the vision of "Estonia 2035" come true! Then in 2035 we can look back to this year and say that we made the right decisions at the time. It's state-of-the-art decisions. They may not always have been the most popular, but they were necessary for Estonia to be the best place in the world to live in any corner of Estonia in 2035, where our people have great opportunities for self-realization, our companies are successful and the tax burden is fair, our elderly are well-maintained, our teachers are well-paid and our country protected, and our allies and partners outside Estonia appreciate us, we are internationally well-known and exemplary in our strong areas. This is how we build Estonia, which matters to the world. Thank you for listening and I am happy to answer the questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U625	The eleventh item on the agenda is the draft law amending the Employment Contracts Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and related other laws 521. The second reading report is presented by Urve Tiidus, member of the Committee on Legal Affairs. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U626	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen, The draft law amending the Employment Contract Act and other related acts with number 521 is today in the main hall at second reading. Between two readings, the Committee on Legal Affairs discussed the draft on 21 March and 4 April. It is recalled that this law is intended to supplement the amount of information to be notified in writing to the staff member before taking up their duties. These amendments are motivated by a Directive of the European Parliament and of the Council on transparent and predictable working conditions in the European Union. There were no amendments to the bill from the Riigikogu. However, the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry and the Estonian Confederation of Trade Unions submitted their opinions and proposals. They were all discussed at length in the Commission on 21 March. I would just like to highlight one topic of discussion. The development of technology and the growing popularity of teleworking make it possible to work with several employers and for much more than 40 hours a week. The impact of this change on the worker's ability to work, the possible increase in risks at work, especially in some occupations, and safety in general, has therefore changed. However, it was quite unanimous that any restrictions on the right to choose work would be detrimental to the fundamental rights of the people. A person needs to know his own limitations. In addition, agreements on restrictions of competition can, of course, be concluded if necessary. Of course, worker protection can also be regulated by less restrictive measures, and this possibility also exists in the law and the draft. Peep Peterson, Chairman of the Confederation of Trade Unions, expressed doubts that labour law would become less favourable to employees if the directive was transposed. However, the Commission's discussion can be summarised by a single question and an answer. Will the rules tighten up when the principles of the directive are introduced into Estonian law? And the answer to this: the Directive deals with the right to employment in a timely manner and specifies the obligation for the employer to notify. Work in practice has already been recorded during the drafting of the draft and no major changes will be made. Here I sum up quoting Seili Suder, the head of the working environment of the Ministry of Social Affairs. The relatively technical draft and current practice will then be clarified in the summary. The Head of the Trade Union Confederation that took part in this meeting stressed the importance of maintaining established practice today. However, on 21 March, the debate gave the draft author an indication that differences of opinion on employment contracts cannot be decided by the Committee on Legal Affairs on what is called a position of power, but require agreement between the social partners. The Commission also gave time for this. On 4 April, the Commission re-convened and took decisions for a second reading. One amendment, one amendment for deletion, was drafted as an initiative of the Committee on Legal Affairs on the draft. This concerns § 5 of the Employment Contracts Act, more specifically the introductory part of the sentence. In short, such a change was made. Initially, it was intended to read as follows: "The employer shall inform the employee in writing of at least the following information before taking up his duties' and a list followed. As the social partners did not agree on this and there is no direct need under the directive, it will remain the way the Employment Contracts Act currently does. Following procedural decisions were taken. Take this amendment fully into account. The consensus was on that. It was further decided to propose that the draft be placed on the agenda on 13 April, that the second reading be closed and that, when the second reading is closed, that the draft be placed on the plenary agenda and that the final vote be held on 20 April. All these decisions were consensual and their consensual decision-makers were Jürgenson, Kannimäe, Kaskpeit, Kruusimäe, Puustusmaa, Tiidus and Toomast. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U628	The 12th item is the draft Act on the ratification of the Agreement between the Government of the Republic of Estonia and the Republic of Austria on the facilitation and protection of investments, initiated by the Government of the Republic of Estonia, 520. We have a first reading. Please ask the Minister of Foreign Affairs of the Riigikogu speech chair Eva-Maria Liimetsa.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U629	Honourable Riigikogu! The [draft] that we are discussing today aims to terminate the bilateral agreement on investment facilitation and protection with Austria. The contract to be concluded was signed on 16 May 1994 in Tallinn and entered into force on 1 October 1995. On 12 January 2021, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Republic of Austria forwarded a note to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Republic of Estonia proposing the termination of the agreement. Similarly to the termination of investment promotion and protection agreements between Finland and Sweden, the termination of this agreement is also necessary in order to ensure equal treatment of investors in all Member States of the European Union under European Union law. Investment promotion and protection agreements concern areas governed by European Union law, in particular the freedom of establishment and the free movement of capital and payments. Since the protection of investment between Member States and the equal treatment of investors are governed by European Union law, bilateral agreements between Member States have no substantive meaning. At the same time, although European Union law prevails over agreements concluded by a Member State, relying on them may nevertheless lead to unequal treatment between investors in Member States, depending on whether or not an investment protection agreement has been concluded. On 15 January 2019, Member States, including Austria, declared that all investment protection agreements within the European Union would be jointly terminated on the basis of a multilateral agreement or bilateral agreements. Austria decided to terminate the investment agreements with the countries of the European Union bilaterally. Estonia has also ratified the bilateral termination agreement with Sweden and Finland, as well as with the United Kingdom that has left the European Union. The termination of the [promotion] bilateral investment agreement with Austria will not reduce investor protection and the continued protection and equal treatment of Estonian and Austrian investors will be ensured by European Union law and European Union internal market rules. Consequently, I would ask the Riigikogu to ratify this [agreement].
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U630	Peter Ernits would like to ask. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U631	Good manager! Minister, thank you very much. You've arrived so fast, you almost missed you. Why Austria has chosen this path, I cannot say. However, in our view, it is important that this legal space and the clarity of the legal space are equally guaranteed to investors. That is why we are proposing to end this bilateral agreement.

The reason is that fundamental changes definitely need a wider debate, and that also means a longer debate, and certainly will not be able to do so in a few months. The amendments proposed by this draft are indeed the most practical problems that have arisen. So certainly, as I said, it is worth looking at the subject more widely in the future. The most critical part of the ATS has been the organisation of competitions. According to the Civil Service Act, posts can be filled by open competition and, in certain justified cases, internal competitions can also be carried out. In particular, competitions have indicated that they are often time-consuming, rigid and bureaucratic. The draft therefore proposes a number of changes that would make this process somewhat more flexible. Firstly, when a temporarily absent official, such as someone on parental leave, states that he will not return to his post. Since the person who applies to him or her has been found to be in a public competition, it is not necessary to organise a new competition in cases where it is established that the person who carries out his or her post on a temporary or fixed-term basis [may continue to work], since he or she already holds the post. What is important is that the skills of this replacement and the work he has done indicate that he really can handle this position, and it can be assumed that if there was a competition and he was involved in it, he would probably be successful. His work so far must show that he is really well suited to this profession. The second option that will be created is the possibility to cancel a competition in a situation where there has been a recent competition with several strong candidates in the administration, and now the authority needs to re-enlist a person in a similar position. It is important, however, that no more than 150 calendar days have passed since the competition and that this initial competition must have a ranking, which can be chosen from among the best three. They can be used, but they don't have to be used. It must always be taken into account that the best possible candidate is appointed for the post. A public competition may therefore be organised at any time and, where justified, an internal competition may also be organised. The draft act also complements the Civil Service Act with a regulation according to which the term of the competition will be suspended by the time the condition for appointment is established. Such an addition is necessary in a situation where, for example, a state secret authorisation is required for taking up a post, but may not be obtained within 120 calendar days of failure of the prescribed competition. And if this authorisation has not been obtained, then the competition may have to be declared unsuccessful under the current regulation and the competition has to be re-established. Another amendment creates the possibility that a partial transfer to another post or another authority may be made by a deadline to be fixed by the official. In this case, it is possible for a person to go on a so-called full-time basis, but now there is a possibility that they will be employed in another institution, and these transfers will also be time-limited. In addition, the Civil Service Act is complemented by a regulation that currently exists in the External Action Service Act and applies only to foreign servants. This is a situation in which an official dies or dies while performing his duties abroad, for [other reason]. In such a situation, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs will arrange to bring dust to Estonia at the expense of the state, if the reimbursement of this cost is not due, for example, to the insurance contract. One amendment to the draft stipulates that the Minister may authorise the establishment of the Ministry's salary guide to the Chancellor. In addition, the Minister is given the opportunity to authorise the establishment of a salary guide for a government agency to the head of a government agency. So far, only the Minister has the right to establish a salary guide. The amendment increases the rights of the head of the governing authority and the Chancellors, but it is a ministerial decision whether or not he wishes to sub-delegate the introduction of a salary guide. It is not mandatory, but one of the possibilities. One amendment to the draft also concerns the right of an official to call for redundancy, in a situation where the position is changed, leading to a change of place of residence or an unreasonable amount of time and money. The amendment to the Unemployment Insurance Act provides a basis for equal treatment of employees and officials in situations where the person is unlawfully released. Under the current regulation, a contract staff member is entitled to unemployment benefit in such a case, but the official does not. This unequal treatment shall be discontinued. The law is intended to enter into force in general. Thank you!

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U680	We can move on to the 14th item, the draft law amending the Penal Code and the Code of Misdemeanour Procedure, initiated by the Government of the Republic, 576, is a first reading. Maris Lauri, the Minister of Justice, will be here as a waitress.
ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U681	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Honourable members of the Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft law amending the Penal Code and the Code of Misdemeanour Procedure (supporting aggression and related hostilities). The draft was sent to the Riigikogu by the Government of the Republic with three fundamental changes, but the Committee on Legal Affairs sent the draft as amended, which is why the draft now deals with two fundamental changes. The first amendment provides for participation in the act of aggression or in its preparation as a criminal offence, and in this connection, Estonia's jurisdiction is also extended in the case of acts committed abroad. This means amendments to § 911 of the Penal Code, respectively, when a new crime formation is created and § 7 (2) is supplemented by section 3. The addition of paragraph 911 creates the possibility of penalising the imminent physical or material support, including funding, of the foreign armed forces or other armed forces involved in the act of aggression, participation in the commission or preparation of the act of aggression or the act of aggression, knowingly, by imprisonment of up to five years. The point of this section is that it will be punished by joining the armed forces of the aggressor or some other armed forces, not even armies, such as state armies, but also private armies, security companies, but also by cooperating with or supporting such aggressor associations or forces, for example, financially or through training or in any other way. This section must be applied in the absence of the offence set out in § 91, or § 91 deals with the punishment of an aggression crime, the management of an act of aggression, the execution of an act of aggression, and it concerns, in particular, the leading persons. Paragraph 911 directs the so-called lower-level players to this and deals with the participation of people by Estonia in the act of aggression. Penalty pursuant to §911 does not preclude the punishment being imposed for other crimes committed in the framework of an act of aggression, in particular war crimes. Since aggression may be committed outside Estonia, the possibility of supplementing § 7 (2) by paragraph 3 is created to punish persons who were citizens of the Republic of Estonia at the time of the act, persons with a residence permit or right of residence in Estonia or legal persons registered in Estonia. The second fundamental change. The justification of an international crime, including by means of symbols, is made punishable as a misdemeanour, i.e. a new paragraph is created, § 1511. More specifically, public display of the symbol used to commit an act of aggression, genocide, crime against humanity or war crime is provided for as a misdemeanour in order to support or justify such acts or to intimidate, humiliate or otherwise seriously disturb another person. The penalties shall be set at a maximum of 300 daily penalty units or arrests for natural persons and a maximum of EUR 32000 for legal persons. An act of aggression, genocide, a crime against humanity or a war crime need not be determined by judgment. These are international crimes, which are international because they are characterised by the fact that they are so-called "world crimes," which are dangerous to the whole of humanity and which are criminalised by the principles of international law. In drawing up the draft, a list of specific symbols was waived. On the one hand, the formation and design of symbols can be a very fast process, one symbol can be replaced by another, so creating a closed list may not be a result. This is also demonstrated by the formation of letter Z as a symbol of Russia's current military aggression. In fact, we cannot rule out at this point that V and O. I myself have observed in video material that the letter "V" is often used in the same position. On the other hand, the message of the symbol depends on the context in which and how it is used. The symbol does not have to have been used directly to commit the crimes mentioned above, but it may have been used, for example, as a definition of the perpetrator's affiliation, as an identity-formator or as a vagina. Public exposure means that it is made visible in public, but not necessarily in public. It can also take place online. The purpose of displaying the symbol may be twofold: to support or justify these crimes, but also to intimidate, humiliate or otherwise significantly disturb another person. This means that exhibiting not every symbol would be punishable. As a proposal, the part of the draft relating to symbols would enter into force within three days of the publication of the Act in the Official Journal of the State. I am also talking about this proposal, which was in the draft submitted by the government, but which was not included in the draft decision by the Committee on Legal Affairs. This proposal concerned the offence of incitement to hatred, or § 151 of the Penal Code. More specifically, it was proposed to eliminate the consequence of the misdemeanour of incitement to hatred, i.e. the threat to a person's life, health or property, but if such consequences had occurred, it would be a criminal offence. I am personally sorry that the Riigikogu found that such changes are not necessary. One could have expected that the connection between what has actually happened in Russia for more than ten years and the direct military aggression against Ukraine will open the eyes of the people in Estonia as well. I know it's happened to a lot of people, but probably not enough. I will once again explain the reasons for this proposal. Firstly, an act of aggression, genocide, crimes against humanity or war crimes can be justified and supported not only by symbols but also by words. When we look at what has happened, the words are much more used and they are also more aggressive. Words are not covered by War propaganda § 92. These crimes are often related to war, but they may not be related to war propaganda. Secondly, words make it much easier to scare, humiliate and disturb people. And, unfortunately, they are also more effective. Thirdly, incitement to hatred involves a large amount of false, even absurd, views, which may result in their initial ridicule and underestimation of its consequences. Words build up and work for a long time. They can be more difficult to identify than symbols that are very visible. The use of words reaches where Russia has reached in relation to Ukraine and the Ukrainians, declaring the Ukrainians a non-existent nation and a non-existent state of Ukraine. With the use of words, Russia has reached the point of justifying genocide without any symbols being used in advance. The symbols came later. We can't subtract symbols from words. Usually, during the design phase of symbols and the aggressive use of symbols, an aggressive, frightening and humiliating word is also important. Perhaps it is more dangerous to wield a symbol than to use words? In the case of words, we assume that there is a consequence where there is a danger to the life, health and property of a person, but in the case of symbols, we are also prepared to ban even if they are only justified or intimidated. I hope the Riigikogu finds time to think. And if not within the framework of this bill, we will soon be ready to be honest and open in the debate on where there is the limit of freedom of expression, what must be done if this limit is exceeded. After all, crossing the boundaries of symbolism, or mentality, is already prepared to intervene with the power of law in our society. Thank you!

The "V" is starting to spread, and he probably doesn't have that kind of scary influence yet, but maybe tomorrow-the day after tomorrow he already does. The letter "A' and others, which have not been used in this context so far, do not yet have [war] implications. But if at one point they're going to have a meaning that they're the bearers of military aggression, then the police will definitely have to intervene. The draft has been drawn up with this idea and with this wording, or at least with an attempt to have a wording that would give us the opportunity to react when these symbols change, change or come up with something else.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U780	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, The Committee on Legal Affairs discussed the draft on Thursday 7 April this year. I name [the following guests]: Roger Kumm, Head of East-Harju Police Department of North Prefecture, Aivar Alavere, Head of the Legal Bureau of the Police and Border Guard Board Aare Hõbe, Advisor to the State Attorney General Kati Tee, Head of the Central Criminal Police of the Police and Border Guard Board Carri Ginter, who has also spoken in the media, and Head of the Penal Law Department of the University of Tartu, co-professor of penal law Anneli Soo was involved with state prosecutor Laura Aiaots. I'm not going to repeat the Minister's report here. The debate in the Committee on Legal Affairs was relatively friendly. Listening to the Minister, the experts involved put forward some questions and opinions. For example, Carri Ginter commented that she is pleased that the topic has reached the table of the Committee on Legal Affairs and will also reach the Riigikogu. He assessed that the debate was also important from the point of view of police work and that the draft procedure should be moved forward. In addition, Mr Ginter stressed that he had the impression from the public discussions that the Minister was prepared to wait on the topic of the hate speech, which has already been heard widely here today. Mr Ginter also commented that the support for aggression against Ukraine was much easier to identify in the case of symbols than in the case of the text. He therefore considered it reasonable to start with the symbols. This is a first step, and then a longer discussion can already be held, who, and on the basis of which, can also prove that there is a pro-linguistic aggression or an incitement to war. Tarmo Kruusimäe, in the Committee on Legal Affairs, stated that the issue could be dealt with directly by taking over the practice of terrorism and applying similar rules. In this case, the situation would be more manageable and more understandable to people, which is allowed and what is not. Carri Ginter also pointed out that the existing and existing law was not sufficient to resolve the present situation. He agreed that the police must make the implementation of the paragraph as simple as possible, and there are two options. The first option is that the policeman must prove that the user of the symbol has the objective of supporting aggression. The second option is to demonstrate through objective monitoring that a person exhibits some symbol in a way that supports aggression. The anti-Semimets wanted to know what steps had been taken with Estonian citizens who went to Donbass and participated in military activities. In addition, he asked whether, in the event of an act of aggression, the person's residence permit would be revoked. Maris Lauri replied that the residence permit could be revoked under the law that is already in force, but that the government needed clarification to make it easier to implement. Mr Poolamets commented that the pentagon is a sensitive issue for Estonians, but military activities are also taking place in other countries, about which foreigners living in Estonia may express their minds. Maris Lauri replied that the context should also be considered when displaying symbols. If someone carries a symbol that is not known in our society, there is no reason to punish the bearer, but there is a reason to punish deliberate provocation. We have also come to the conclusion that Commissioner Vilja Toomas expressed the opinion that this draft should be adopted under an accelerated procedure. He added that he supported the extended version of § 1511 presented by the Government of the Republic, as in this case the law can be interpreted as necessary. He also expressed his support for the deletion of § 151 from the draft as it requires a longer discussion and it is important to move forward quickly with the procedure at the moment. The anti-Semimets also commented that the abstractness of the draft text, the degree of generalisation, is important and should not lead to a situation in which, at the end of the war, the adopted amendments to the law should in turn be abolished. Consequently, as chairman of the committee, I proposed to refer the draft to the first reading of 576 in an amended form, excluding from the draft text the amendment of § 1 (3) of the draft, which amends § 151 of the Penal Code, incitement to hatred. The members of the Commission were fully in agreement on this. At this point, I proposed that draft 576 be referred to first reading. What time is it? Yeah, 23.54. So we barely made it on April 13. I proposed ending the first reading of the draft and setting the deadline for amendments at 18 April, Monday morning at 10 a.m. The members of the Commission did not want to vote and were consensual on this matter. However, we also suggested from the Commission that, when the first reading of draft 576 is closed, the draft should be referred to second reading on 20 April, and that when the second reading is closed, the draft should be put to the final vote on 21 April. Here too, the Members of the Commission did not wish to vote and were in full agreement. We also made a decision to appoint Marek Jürgenson as rapporteur for the Commission. There was a complete consensus on this, too. So the procedural decisions have been read to you here, and such important moments of what took place in the Commission have also been highlighted. So I'd be happy to answer your questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-04-13_U785	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Good fellows! If you take a look from the Nordic world, you can't deny it: it's an alarming time for us and for others. In the Estonian Human Rights Centre's newsletter, we read: "The enemy speech [but] humiliates and humiliates people because of their skin colour, ethnic origin, nationality, language, religious persuasion, gender, gender identity, sexual orientation, disability or age. Hostile speech can be found anywhere on the street and on the Internet. Hostile speech may be anonymous, but you can hear it from someone you know or even from politicians. The most terrifying thing is that hate speech encourages intolerance and violence, creating an environment in which discrimination against and attacking certain people would seem justified, even if speaking is not directly called for violence." And more. We have agreed with Europe, along with other European countries, that public incitement to violence is criminally punishable. Unfortunately, Estonia has not respected the agreement, being the only ones in Europe with Romania. Poverty clearly involves a psychological attack, violence, or a call to it. Public hatred grows easily into action, and then it is too late to hold back violence or control it. It mobilises people who would not be active without such calls. When people are called upon to hate a person based on his group membership or are called upon to commit violence against a particular group, nothing will happen today. This means that there is no regulation at the level of the law. In order to be condemnable and punishable, it must have clear, qualified features. In society, we must, of course, negotiate this issue more, so that people can understand that anger alone is not yet animosity. Instead, if hate speech is not dealt with in society, malicious insults may be overcome by acts of property abuse or even physical violence. If someone doesn't do anything, it'll make it look like they might do it. Thank you.
